*Lalchand Pandia, Kanishka Misra, Allyson Ettinger*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Selecting high-quality training data can reduce computation cost for LLM fine-tuning. Prior work on data selection developed a variety of difficulty-based scores, motivated by the intuition that harder examples are more informative. While effective for smaller classification models, we find that such methods often fail to outperform random selection for LLM fine-tuning. Our analysis reveals that random selection performs better because it is more representative of the underlying input space---i.e., has better coverage. To validate the importance of coverage, we implement a simple clustering-based selection method which selects data that is more representative of the underlying input distribution. On a set of diverse single-domain tasks, this method yields performance at or above that of random selection. On the diverse, multi-domain Tülu 3 mixture, where random sampling no longer guarantees a balanced subset, our method outperforms all methods. Overall, our results indicate that prioritizing coverage over difficulty leads to more effective data selection for LLM fine-tuning.


### [48] [Permanent and Transient Representations for Continual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07327)
*Nishanth Anand, Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continual Reinforcement Learning (CRL) agents struggle to adapt to new situations while retaining prior knowledge, leading to a stability-plasticity trade-off. One approach to solving this problem is by using complementary learning systems: one for slow, long-term learning and another for quick, transient adaptation. Anand & Precup (2023) instantiated this idea using permanent and transient value functions, but used the same features in both systems. Intuitively, long-term learning should rely more on parametric representations for broad generalization, while transient learning would be best served by non-parametric representations for situation-specific adaptation. In this paper, we explore this idea both theoretically and empirically. By introducing a novel non-parametric approximation for estimating the transient value function in complex tasks, we demonstrate that our method enables online learning and outperforms competitive baselines on image-based tasks and Craftax-Classic, underscoring the effectiveness of our system-level decomposition.


### [49] [AtlasULP: Domain-aware Universal Link Prediction via Relation Atlas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07347)
*Yujing Liu, Yixin Liu, Yu Zheng, Lianhua Chi, Alan Wee-Chung Liew, Heng Tao Shen, Shirui Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Link prediction (LP) is a widely applied task in graph learning. Conventional LP approaches typically follow a dataset-specific paradigm, requiring independent training for each graph and incurring high computational and maintenance costs in large-scale applications. Motivated by these limitations, recent work explores Universal Link Prediction (ULP), aiming to enable training-free inference on arbitrary unseen graphs. However, existing methods primarily rely on subgraph sampling strategies to construct universal representations, which are unable to capture higher-order information due to the exponential growth of higher-order neighborhoods. As a result, they often under-perform on graphs where long-range signals are essential, such as biological networks. Moreover, their prediction models are typically domain-agnostic and lack the ability to adapt to different graph domains, leading to limited generalization. To address these challenges, we propose an Atlas-based Universal Link Prediction framework (AtlasULP), which introduces the relation atlas to capture structural associations between node pairs from both global and local perspectives, enabling a comprehensive characterization of connectivity signals across diverse graph domains. Building upon this representation, we develop a domain-guided prompting ULP model, which generates domain-aware prompt tokens from contextual structures and performs prompt-guided in-context prediction for adaptive link inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AtlasULP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across diverse graph domains.


### [50] [Acoustic Anomaly Detection for Early Identification of Drill Jamming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07356)
*Maria Beatriz Silva, Lucas Edson Silva de Araújo, Sergio F. Chevtchenko, Ermeson Andrade, Danilo Araujo, Saeed Afshar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In the context of Industry 4.0, predictive maintenance is essential to minimize unplanned downtime and ensure operational safety. In machining processes, drill jamming is a critical failure that compromises both workpiece quality and tool integrity. This work proposes an acoustic anomaly detection system using ultrasonic sensors to identify pre-failure patterns in Computer Numerical Control (CNC) machining. We release a public dataset of time- and frequency-domain acoustic features from 17 drills, and benchmark semi-supervised autoencoder-based normality modelling against supervised XGBoost classification using Leave-One-Drill-Out validation. The results demonstrate that the Autoencoder is highly effective for industrial safety, achieving a Recall of 1.00 and a consolidated F1-score of 0.81. Furthermore, the threshold-independent analysis shows that the Autoencoder better separates normal and pre-failure acoustic patterns, achieving a consolidated AUC of 0.8294, compared with 0.7559 for the supervised model under inter-tool covariate shift. In contrast, the supervised XGBoost achieved an average F1-score of 0.55 and failed to detect failures in some of the evaluated drills. The findings highlight that the ultrasonic spectrum provides a reliable window for identifying incipient mechanical instabilities, and the AE model offers a robust safety envelope by anticipating all jamming events across the tested drill bits.


### [51] [Test-Time Self-Play: Scaling without a Verifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07367)
*Akira Yoshiyama, Akmal Ashirmatov, Jonas Hübotter, Ido Hakimi, Andreas Krause*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Test-time scaling methods spend additional compute at deployment to improve a large language model's performance on a hard target problem. Most methods rely on a verifier that scores candidate solutions for correctness, using it to select among or refine the model's outputs. In many deployment regimes, however, a strong verifier on the target problem is unavailable. We introduce Test-Time Self-Play (TTSP), an approach to test-time scaling using asymmetric self-play without reliance on a verifier. On a difficulty-calibrated subset of LiveCodeBench v6, TTSP is the best-performing method at every verifier strength we evaluate, and the gap to the strongest baseline widens as the verifier weakens; in the no-verifier limit, TTSP yields a 35\% absolute improvement in mean@8. The gains hold across a range of verifier strengths, except when the base policy has near-zero coverage of the correct answer.


### [52] [When Scores Conflict with Preferences: Pre-Alignment Diagnosis and Adaptive Correction of Constraint Drift in DPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07375)
*Ping Liu, Yan Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is increasingly used in settings where pairwise preferences coexist with pointwise criteria such as response length, answer correctness, or safety scores. When the two disagree, standard DPO treats pairs whose preferred output scores worse on the pointwise criterion (conflict pairs) in the same way as the remaining (matching) pairs. We characterize a systematic failure mode, constraint drift: for sufficiently small updates, conflict pairs tend to increase constraint cost while matching pairs tend to repair it, and the net change depends on their balance. This balance yields a closed-form pre-alignment diagnostic, $\rho^{*}_{\mathrm{eq}}$, computable from dataset statistics, that indicates whether a dataset is self-correcting or requires intervention. Building on this diagnostic, we propose Conflict-aware DPO (CoDPO), which down-weights conflict pairs with a strength set from $\rho^{*}_{\mathrm{eq}}$ rather than tuned, and adds an adaptive margin shift that addresses residual drift during training. Empirically, drift grows monotonically with conflict exposure on the conflict-dominated domains, and the intervention decision given by $\rho^{*}_{\mathrm{eq}}$ matches the observed drift on every domain, including one that it identifies as self-correcting. On all four domains of the main comparison, CoDPO reduces drift while largely preserving the preference learning of vanilla DPO. Compared with hard filtering and recent constrained and multi-objective baselines, CoDPO also achieves a more favorable drift-preference trade-off.


### [53] [Adversarial Multiplicity: Cherry-Picked Models and the Limits of Disclosure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07378)
*Cassandra C. Chou, Benjamin Q. Huynh, Allison Koenecke, Elizabeth T. Chin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Predictive multiplicity -- where similarly accurate models disagree on individual predictions -- can be leveraged to optimize secondary metrics (e.g., fairness) without loss in predictive performance. However, exploiting multiplicity opens the opportunity for motivated parties (or coding agents gaming their objectives) to steer predictions toward their own ends, such as in litigation or consumer disputes with automated decision-making tools. In these adversarial multiplicity settings, we distinguish soft manipulation, where parties optimize over innocuous model choices (e.g., hyperparameter tuning, feature engineering), from hard manipulation, where parties directly perturb model parameters. We find a sufficiently flexible model can be manipulated to reverse any individual prediction with minimal change in predictive performance while passing black-box audits, making hard manipulation viable in most regulatory settings. Soft manipulation has smaller effects but can pass white-box audits requiring full access to code and data. Even aggregate estimates can be manipulated: a model's estimated effect of climate change on wildfires can be fully reversed by hard edits and soft search. We find the Reconcile algorithm typically resolves most disagreements between models, but more often in favor of the manipulator when the opposing model is chosen adversarially compared to at random. We map which manipulations can plausibly pass existing regulations and disclosure policies, and recommend mitigations such as pre-registration, proof of model training, sensitivity analyses, multi-metric evaluations, and committing to versioned public models.


### [54] [Spectral symmetry fingerprints in equivariant representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07410)
*Jiacheng Cen, Anyi Li, Ning Lin, Zihe Wang, Ji-Rong Wen, Wenbing Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Symmetry governs the organization of physical structures, while symmetry breaking reveals their transformations. In geometric deep learning, symmetry imposes rank constraints on equivariant representations, limiting the expressive capacity of equivariant graph neural networks (GNNs). Here we show that these constraints can instead be exploited: the singular-value spectra of equivariant representations provide a unified fingerprint of structural symmetry. We introduce spectral symmetry fingerprints (SymFP), which enable discrete point-group identification through rank-template matching and continuous symmetry analysis by quantifying spectral energy beyond target rank constraints. SymFP is orientation invariant and architecture agnostic, enabling a parameter-free GNN implementation. Its spectra can be reused across multiple target groups, and the resulting leakage scores satisfy subgroup monotonicity. Across synthetic structures, molecules, coordination complexes and large biological assemblies, SymFP accurately identifies point groups, tracks controlled symmetry breaking and reveals local and degree-resolved symmetry changes that can be obscured by global geometric measures. These results establish the spectral structure of equivariant representations as a general framework for characterizing symmetry across physical systems.


### [55] [IMUDINO: Self-distilled Foundation Model for Inertial Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07437)
*Zhoutong Ye, Chengwen Zhang, Zixuan Wang, Xufeng Jian, Zhe He, Qingyang Wan, Xutong Wang, Jiaqi Liu, Mingze Sun, Yu Mei, Chun Yu, Yuanchun Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We present IMUDINO, the first foundation model (FM) for inertial sensing to achieve competitive performance across diverse sensing tasks including human activity recognition (HAR), health, motion capture, and odometry. Prior inertial FMs often focus on one subfield of inertial sensing and sometimes underperform state-of-the-art supervised methods (e.g., in HAR), making their scope considerably narrower than FMs for vision and natural language. Moreover, they often require fixed input length and sampling rate, and generalize poorly to unseen sensor locations. To build a general-purpose foundation model for inertial sensing, we adapt the DINO self-supervised learning (SSL) paradigm to inertial data through a set of semantics-preserving motion transformations that simulate variations in motion pattern and sensor setup. For model architecture, we use a plain transformer encoder with frequency-aware positional encoding that natively supports varying input length and sampling rate. This simple setup proved remarkably effective for learning global and dense features generalizable across users, sensor setups, tasks, and, in the case of robot odometry, embodiments. We hope IMUDINO can serve as a backbone for inertial sensing and kickstart the development of general-purpose inertial FMs.


### [56] [Two-stage residual learning for calibrated uncertainty in solar irradiance forecasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07444)
*RR Flanagan, E Kasha, PW Talbot, AS Epiny, MR Deinert*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate forecasting of renewable generation requires not only point predictions but also reliable estimates of the uncertainty attached to them, particularly at extended horizons. Machine learning models are increasingly used to predict solar output, but they typically report a single value and capture the associated uncertainty poorly. The standard remedy is to train a model to predict the parameters of an assumed probability distribution directly. Here we develop an alternative two-stage method, motivated by synthetic solar signal generation, in which the forecast error is modeled separately from the expected irradiance. A first long short-term memory (LSTM) network predicts the expected global horizontal irradiance, and a second network models the distribution of the forecast error, which is added back to the mean to give both the forecast and its uncertainty. Using historical weather data from the National Solar Radiation Database and a probability integral transform evaluation, we show that explicitly modeling the error brings the predicted standard deviation markedly closer to the historical value, reducing the deviation between the two by 34.6 percent for a standalone LSTM and by 56.5 percent when the LSTM is coupled to Gaussian process regression, the most reliable of the configurations tested. Because the method models the total forecast error directly, it captures the full predictive uncertainty that matters for decisions, rather than only the model contribution that dropout-based approaches estimate.


### [57] [Echoes of Error: Residual-Directional Local Rollout Consistency for Timeseries Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07473)
*Zheng Wang, Kaixuan Zhang, Xiaonan Lu, Wanfang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In time-series forecasting, rollout errors are not merely one-step fitting discrepancies: once fed back into the evolving state, they can perturb future predictions. We formalize this effect through local rollout consistency, which compares the next prediction produced under teacher forcing with that produced under free running. In principle, this consistency can be enforced directly by tracking the residual-induced perturbation of the next state. In practice, however, the residual-to-state alignment operator is often difficult to design when the input state and output block have different dimensions, heterogeneous components, or architecture-dependent layouts. We therefore use a first-order local surrogate that penalizes the Jacobian-vector product of the predictor along the actual feedback perturbation, suppressing precisely the directions through which self-generated errors remain visible to future predictions. We further give the same loss a geometric interpretation near a predictive manifold: by controlling residual-induced directions that probe the normal bundle, the objective yields theoretical conditions under which free-running trajectories remain inside a stability tube around the manifold. Experiments on time series forecasting tasks support this view, showing that the proposed regularization improves predictive accuracy. More broadly, our results suggest a training principle for time-series forecasters and sequential predictors: a model should not only make small one-step errors, but also learn not to let its own errors echo into the future. Code is available at this https URL .


### [58] [Adversarial Robustness of Kernel Machines: A Comprehensive Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07474)
*Rahul Kumar Yadav, Tunir Ghosh, Sudhendu Ahir, Parthe Pandit, Arjun Bhagoji*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Kernel machines are an important class of models in machine learning. However, the question of how robust modern kernel machines are to adversarial examples has not been studied comprehensively. In this paper, we provide a thorough empirical investigation of the adversarial robustness of kernel-based models for both binary and multiclass classification tasks, via white-box and black-box evasion attacks. We evaluate the native robustness of kernel machines, random feature classifiers, and the relatively newer Recursive Feature Machines. In addition, we provide an algorithm for approximate adversarial training of kernel machines. Our experiments on classification tasks on image and tabular datasets strongly indicate that kernel machines are natively more robust than neural networks, i.e., their performance degrades more gracefully against adversarial perturbations.


### [59] [Localization Does Not Imply Steerability: A Mechanistic Study of Sign Errors in LLM Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07478)
*Shradha Agarwal, Tariq J., Deepak Rajbhar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: When large language models do multi-step mathematics, a characteristic failure recurs: the model writes one wrong sign token mid-computation -- a cofactor sign, the minus of a recursive integration-by-parts step -- then executes the rest of the arithmetic correctly, and the answer comes out wrong. We ask where inside the network that sign is written. Across six open models spanning 14B to 675B parameters, a per-layer readout shows the wrong sign is committed late -- by MLP layers in the last quarter of the network (70-100% per model and task), a pattern present, where tested (Qwen), in the base model before instruction tuning. The mechanism varies: some models carry fixed "minus-stamp" layers that push a minus regardless of the right answer, others carry amplifiers of whichever sign was already chosen, and Qwen adds a downstream rescue layer that opposes the stamp. The circuit is causal: subtracting a single sign direction from the residual stream flips 13 of 14 held-out minus-written integration-by-parts errors on Llama-3.3-70B, with flips replicating on Qwen, Gemma, and Mistral-Small (24-70% per cell), and with representation-level specificity -- the same dose breaks correctly-minus answers while sparing correct-plus answers where the direction is surgical (0/72 on Llama), and matched random-direction and control-layer interventions are null. Core claims were preregistered with pass criteria frozen in git before held-out data was seen: 23 of 29 checks passed, all misses reported. But causality is not uniform: Phi-4 implements an equally well-localized circuit at the same late depth, yet the identical intervention flips almost nothing -- the sign is written redundantly across ~15 layers with margins several times larger (median 17.8 logits). Localization, we conclude, does not imply steerability: two models can share a circuit location yet differ enormously in how overturnable it is.


### [60] [Learning Multi-Agent Orchestration:Higher-Order Interactions in Policy Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07495)
*Kaixuan Liu, Guojun Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Learned orchestration has emerged as a promising paradigm for large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems, in which a central orchestrator selects agents and controls the information they receive throughout a task. Despite rapid empirical progress, existing methods train the orchestrator by crediting each decision with its own advantage, implicitly assuming that the other decisions in the episode stay fixed. This assumption fails in orchestration, where the contribution of each decision to the final outcome depends on the decisions preceding it, so the gain of a joint update is not the sum of its per-decision effects. To address this challenge, we introduce \textbf{IAPO}, an interaction-aware policy optimization framework that explicitly accounts for interactions among decisions. Specifically, IAPO exactly decomposes the performance difference between two policies into the effects of individual decisions and their interactions, which we prove can account for half of the attainable gain, and then optimizes both from sampled trajectories while certifying that every update is an improvement. On long-input versions of GSM8K, MATH, DROP, MBPP and HumanEval, against fifteen published multi-agent and workflow-search systems on the same 7B executor, IAPO's deployed plans reach the strongest system's accuracy with fewer tokens, and removing the interaction term costs 7 to 17 points of accuracy on code.


### [61] [RLGPA: A Reinforcement Learning-Guided Granularity Planning Attack for Decision-Based Sparse Adversarial Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07512)
*Chao Zhou, Shenghua He, Jiaxin Mo, Qiangguo Yu, Fengchuang Xing*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Decision-based sparse attacks must reduce perturbation support using only the victim's predicted label. The best restoration strategy varies as support contracts: coarse removal works early, while tail states need finer, history-aware tests. We propose RLGPA, an online reinforcement learning method that schedules support-reduction executors and their granularity under a hard query budget. A masked Dueling Double DQN selects among seven stable policy--scale actions; each action produces support-relative restoration groups that are rebuilt after every macro decision. A feasibility-preserving executor commits only adversarial restorations and feeds support gain and query cost back to the planner. Under the matched top-1-label oracle, RLGPA reaches 61\% targeted ASR at sparsity 0.01 on CIFAR-10 (vs. 25\% SparseEvo, 0\% Pointwise), and 75.5\%/73.0\% on ResNet50/ViT at sparsity 0.005 (vs. 29.0\%/15.5\%). A fixed-probe test reduces median \(L_0\) from 165 to 23 after 40 images without any update. State-blind and state-shuffled controls degrade significantly, confirming that the learned policy uses attack state; the main gains come from state-conditioned executor scheduling and dynamic repartition. The source code is publicly available at this https URL .


### [62] [Understanding Data Influence in Transportation Networks: A Physics-Constrained Influence-Function Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07526)
*Hamidreza Attari, Yueyue Fan, Johannes Royset*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Understanding how individual training observations influence model predictions is important for developing reliable and interpretable data-driven transportation systems. Existing influence-function methods, however, are primarily formulated for unconstrained learning and do not explicitly account for physical constraints that couple different components of a transportation network. Under such constraints, perturbing a training observation can affect model parameters and predictions only through physically feasible directions, potentially changing how its influence propagates across the network. This paper develops a hard physics-informed influence-function framework for constrained learning, in which training-data influence is characterized within the physically feasible space defined by network constraints. The analytical results reveal how physical constraints alter the magnitude, direction, and spatial distribution of training-sample influence relative to conventional influence measures. Using traffic prediction under flow-conservation constraints as a case study, we show that influential observations identified by the proposed framework differ systematically from those identified by classical influence functions. Through orthogonal decomposition, we quantitatively characterize how much of a perturbation in the training data is propagated through the network via the flow-conservation constraints and how much is removed by the constraint projection. The proposed influence framework provides a physically consistent approach for evaluating training-data influence in constrained transportation learning problems, with potential applications to data diagnostics, sensor evaluation, and targeted data collection.


### [63] [Provable Zeroth-Order Safe Exploration for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07561)
*Weiqin Chen, Xinmeng Huang, Dongsheng Ding, Santiago Paternain*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning from human feedback (Safe-RLHF) seeks to improve helpfulness while satisfying harmlessness constraints. Existing primal-dual approaches can converge to feasible policies but do not prevent constraint violations during training. We study a stronger requirement, safe exploration for RLHF, under which every policy executed during training, including auxiliary exploration policies, remains feasible. We propose Zeroth-Order Log-Barrier Policy Gradient (ZO-LBPG), a reward-model-free method that optimizes a log-barrier objective using zeroth-order estimates derived directly from human preferences. To ensure safety under noisy feedback, we combine conservative constraint margin estimates with coupled rollouts and an adaptive step size that prevents updates from crossing constraint boundaries. We prove that, with high probability, all iterates and exploration policies remain strictly feasible, and that ZO-LBPG globally converges to an approximately optimal constrained policy. We establish a sample complexity of $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\varepsilon^{-6}+d^3\varepsilon^{-5})$ trajectories and $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\varepsilon^{-8}+d^5\varepsilon^{-7})$ human queries. Experiments on a synthetic CMDP and a language-model alignment task corroborate the training-time safety guarantees.


### [64] [MambaTG: Candidate-Aware State Space Modeling for Future Link Prediction on Temporal Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07569)
*Nguyen Hong Quan, Sitao Luan, Yilun Zheng, Lihui Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Future link prediction on temporal graphs involves both repeated interactions between previously connected node pairs and first-time interactions between pairs with no prior interaction. While repeated-edge prediction can exploit direct pairwise evidence, unseen-edge prediction must generalize beyond historical recurrence. Existing temporal graph models often exploit signals tied to recurring interactions or historical overlap, which are highly informative for repeated edges but become limited or unavailable for unseen edges, contributing to substantial performance degradation on unseen-edge prediction. Based on this observation, we identify two complementary signals for modeling sequential dynamics beyond historical recurrence: (1) historical-sequence evolution, which captures dependencies among the source node's chronologically ordered interactions, and (2) candidate--history compatibility, which captures how well a candidate fits the source node's evolving history. Based on the decomposition, we propose MambaTG, a candidate-aware Mamba-based framework that jointly models these two signals. Mamba provides a natural mechanism for this design: its selective recurrence captures the evolution of ordered interaction histories, while its input-dependent state transitions allow the history state to adapt to different candidate inputs. Extensive experiments on 13 temporal graph datasets against 13 representative baselines show that MambaTG achieves strong and consistent performance across both repeated and unseen edges while remaining computationally efficient.


### [65] [ACSeL: Adaptive Configuration Search for Hardware-Constrained LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07570)
*Bokun Wang, Hung-Yueh Chiang, Diana Marculescu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Serving large language models (LLMs) under tight memory and latency service-level objectives (SLOs), including on edge devices, requires jointly selecting the model size, batch size, quantization algorithm, and mixed-precision quantization recipes. Exhaustive search over this combinatorially large space is infeasible. Unlike data-center serving, which often prioritizes throughput or generation speed, resource-constrained deployments typically set memory and latency budgets as constraints and then seek the configuration with the highest generation quality among feasible ones. To this end, we propose ACSeL, a framework that identifies configurations predicted to satisfy deployment constraints while achieving high predicted generation quality. To enable efficient quality estimation, we use Relative Model Quality Degradation (RMQD), a proxy based on the divergence between the candidate and reference models' next-token distributions over a small calibration corpus. Across the evaluated model sizes and precisions, RMQD is inexpensive to evaluate and strongly correlated with downstream benchmark performance. ACSeL combines (i) a lightweight quality surrogate that predicts RMQD directly from a configuration; (ii) Few-shot Median Calibration (FMC), which calibrates operator-profile-based latency estimates using a small number of end-to-end profiles; and (iii) a structured search algorithm BandPareto for exploring layerwise mixed-precision configurations. Experiments on an NVIDIA RTX A6000 workstation and an NVIDIA Jetson Thor edge platform show that FMC substantially improves deployment feasibility, while BandPareto + FMC achieves the highest feasibility and the lowest RMQD gaps to the best measured configurations in the evaluation set.


### [66] [Binding Paraphrased Instructions to Encountered Objects in sLLM Agents under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07599)
*Chanyoung Kim, Harim Song, Byoungchan Ko, Keonyoung Lee, Myungwon Lee, Dahuin Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reliable deployment of LLM agents requires grounding diverse user expressions in their environments. Although RL-finetuned smaller large language model (sLLM) agents rival larger prompted LLMs on embodied tasks, their robustness to instruction paraphrases remains unclear. We introduce ALFWorld-Para to evaluate this robustness under controlled paraphrases, revealing 21-52% declines in success rates (SRs), predominantly driven by object synonyms. Despite retaining knowledge of synonym referents, agents often fail to take the corresponding objects even after observing them, exposing a gap between referential knowledge and object binding. Partial observability further complicates binding, as referents may remain unseen or have already been encountered under different labels. To address this challenge, we propose BINDIntent, a training-free framework that probabilistically binds referring expressions to observed labels using semantic proximity and estimated persistence against unseen candidates. Without additional training or LLM calls, BINDIntent improves synonym-paraphrase success of RL-finetuned sLLMs by 17.5-49.1 percentage points (pp) with only 0.4 ms of CPU overhead per step. It also raises GiGPO-7B's overall SR from 61.8% to 77.4%, surpassing a prompted 24B LLM. Code and benchmark are available at this https URL .


### [67] [Monotone Viability Learning for Offline Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07616)
*Ziqian Wang, Zhen Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Offline safe reinforcement learning requires reliable long-horizon safety estimates from fixed data. Learning Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) viability in this setting calls for semantic preservation, convergence, and numerical stability. To address these challenges, we propose Monotone Viability Learning (MoViL), which learns viable sets from offline transitions through monotone finite-horizon set recursion. The recursion preserves finite-horizon safety semantics and converges to the infinite-horizon viability kernel. Binary supervision maintains a fixed target scale across horizons, avoiding discount-induced numerical attenuation. We further establish sufficient conditions for recovering behavior-supported viable subsets. The learned viability estimates are used to certify actions at deployment. Dubins car experiments validate the numerical accuracy of the learned viable sets. Evaluations on safe RL benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art reward-cost performance. MoViL satisfies the average cost constraint on every task and achieves the lowest average cost among the compared methods. These results highlight MoViL's potential to extend long-horizon HJ viability analysis to safety-critical systems where reliable dynamics models are unavailable and online exploration is unsafe.


### [68] [Online Learning with Ordered Experts (via Two-point Feedback)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07629)
*Shiliang Zuo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study adversarial online learning with expert recommendations in a totally ordered action space, where the experts' recommendations may change and be reordered arbitrarily. Our focus is two-point feedback: on each round the learner plays two current recommendations, observes both, and pays their average. We study two problems. In the separated monotone problem, the loss is the sum of a hidden nondecreasing component and a hidden nonincreasing component, and a query reveals the two components separately. In the BV problem, the losses are bounded and have bounded variation (BV) along the order, and a query reveals only the loss. In the separated monotone problem, two paid observations achieve \[ O\bigl(\log(|\cF|T)\sqrt{T\log|\cF|}\bigr) \] regret against the best of $|\cF|$ experts, within a logarithmic factor of the classical full-information bound. For the BV problem, we obtain $\wtO(\sqrt T+|\cF|)$ regret. We give an application in dynamic pricing with expert advice, showing that two-option menus matches the full information rate up to $\log$ factors. The main technical ingredient is a marginal-preserving endpoint flow estimator. Two observations reveal a loss increment along the order; random paths supply telescoping identities that turn this local information into unbiased loss estimates for all current recommendations. A reversible pair sampler preserves the learner's intended marginal at both played actions. The estimator's second-moment and stability bounds support online updates for both feedback models.


### [69] [Measuring the Marginal Value of Verifier-Based RL Beyond Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07639)
*Amit Sagtani, Saloni Garg*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is usually evaluated as an alternative to supervised fine-tuning (SFT), or as a stage that follows it, with the implicit assumption that the RL stage contributes gains of its own. We measure that contribution directly, in a controlled setting where both stages can be run to completion and compared exactly. We built gpu-train-debug, a deterministic benchmark of 1,877 synthetic GPU and LLM training failure diagnosis tasks across 30 archetypes, graded by a programmatic verifier with partial credit, and calibrated so that a mid-tier model scores in a 0.60 to 0.75 band. A pilot produced a surprise: 1,200 gold demonstrations nearly solved the benchmark (SFT 0.785 on the hard split), while 15 steps of GRPO from the base model did not move it (0.314 vs 0.311 base). We therefore amended the study, in writing and before the full run, to ask the sharper question: what does verifier-based RL add beyond demonstrations? At the pre-registered scale (60 GRPO steps, learning rate 1e-6), the answer is: no detectable improvement in-distribution (+0.22 points, 95% CI [-0.06, +0.51]), and a modest but measurable improvement under distribution shift (+0.33 points on a held-out OOD split, CI [+0.02, +0.68]). SFT accounted for most of the available improvement, while verifier-based RL produced only modest additional gains under distribution shift. The residual errors SFT leaves are mostly systematic, identical across samples, which likely reduces the within-group learning signal available to GRPO. A full 60-step cold-start GRPO arm confirms the pilot at the full horizon. The whole study cost $8.69 of compute and is fully reproducible from archived generations, frozen configs, and tagged code.


### [70] [WaSP: Wasserstein Data Synthesis Pipeline for Solver-Free Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07646)
*Ryan Liu, Chuwei Wang, Ricardo Baptista, Andrew Stuart, Anima Anandkumar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural operators offer fast surrogates for solving PDEs, but training them typically requires expensive input-solution pairs. Two solver-free alternatives include physics-informed learning and training on manufactured data. Physics-informed learning approximates solutions by minimizing PDE residuals. While the residual norm must be strong enough to control solution error, stronger norms increase mini-batch variance and hinder optimization. Manufactured data constructs input-solution pairs for learning by applying the PDE operator to artificial solutions. However, the resulting training distribution can be mutually singular with the target distribution, yielding poor accuracy on the target distribution. We introduce the Wasserstein Data Synthesis Pipeline (WaSP), which trains on manufactured input-solution pairs while gradually mitigating the distribution shift. WaSP synthesizes new pairs by applying the learned operator to a path of intermediate distributions converging to the target input distribution. Training proceeds in stages along this path. At each stage, the previous surrogate generates solutions, and the exact PDE operator derives corresponding inputs, yielding training pairs for the next surrogate. Theoretically, we prove a stepwise error-propagation bound for the distribution shift introduced during synthesis. Empirically, WaSP reduces solution error by $3.9\times$ to $9.5\times$ compared to existing solver-free methods on PDEs such as Allen-Cahn, Kuramoto-Sivashinsky, and Ginzburg-Landau, and better reproduces the two chaotic systems' statistical observables.


### [71] [Simple Meta-Learning for HPO and CASH Using Performance Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07659)
*Emiel Witting, Cheng Yan, Tom Julian Viering*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Bayesian optimization for Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Combined Algorithm Selection and Hyperparameter Optimization (CASH) often relies on explicit hyperparameter encodings as surrogate inputs. In high-dimensional search spaces, however, such representations can make it difficult for standard surrogates to learn useful performance models. We instead study an alternative meta-learning representation that describes each configuration directly as its historical performance across prior tasks. This representation can be paired directly with standard surrogate models. Our scope is the iterative selection component of AutoML, deciding which configuration to evaluate next from a fixed candidate set, rather than a complete framework with features such as runtime estimation, cost-aware budgeting and ensembling. We further apply this representation to maximal marginal relevance (MMR), a diversity-based re-ranking criterion from recommender systems, as a posterior-free acquisition rule. When selecting and optimising across multiple models on the TabArena benchmark, the performance-based representation paired with a linear Bayesian ridge regression model outperforms dedicated meta-learning baselines, achieving the same tuned performance in roughly half the number of trials. For single-model HPO, it remains a competitive second. Across both benchmarks this method closely matches or improves on regret at a fraction of the complexity and cost, requiring no pre-training or tuning and scaling independently of search space dimensionality. Code for reproducing our results is available at this https URL .


### [72] [An Information Bottleneck for Post-hoc Memorization Mitigation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07669)
*Dina EL Zein, Shashi Kumar, James Henderson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent studies have shown that large language models (LLMs) can memorize and reproduce copyrighted training data, raising serious privacy and intellectual property concerns. In this work, we investigate whether memorization can be mitigated without modifying or retraining the underlying model through an information bottleneck. We propose a modular plug-and-play framework that inserts a nonparametric variational information bottleneck (NVIB) between a frozen LLM backbone and its language modeling head. By compressing the final hidden representations before token prediction, the proposed bottleneck aims to preserve general linguistic knowledge while filtering out highly specific memorized sequences. We evaluate our approach on memorization extraction tasks involving book passages and on standard language modeling benchmarks to measure the trade-off between memorization reduction and downstream utility. Our experiments show that the NVIB layer substantially reduces extractability while maintaining competitive performance on benchmark tasks. These results suggest that adding an information bottleneck offers a practical and architecture agnostic direction for post-hoc memorization mitigation in pretrained LLMs.


### [73] [Calibrated Target Noise Recovers Curvature from the Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07671)
*Arash Jamshidi, Katsiaryna Haitsiukevich, Aristides Gionis, Kai Puolamäki*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Estimating curvature information from gradients alone is a fundamental problem in machine learning. The challenge becomes particularly acute in settings where only aggregate mini-batch gradients are available, yet curvature information is still required for applications such as preconditioning in optimisation, sampling, and early stopping. Perhaps surprisingly, we show that a simple target-perturbation scheme suffices to recover such curvature information from gradients alone. Our method injects zero-mean noise into the targets, with variance calibrated to the local output curvature. The covariance of the resulting noisy batch gradients then recovers practical curvature matrices, without requiring per-sample gradients, second-order derivatives, or other internal network quantities. For a broad class of non-linear models, our scheme recovers the Generalised Gauss--Newton (GGN) matrix; for feed-forward ReLU networks, it additionally recovers the diagonal blocks of the population Hessian. We demonstrate that the resulting estimator is simple, lightweight, provably optimal, and easy to integrate into existing training pipelines. Experiments demonstrate accurate curvature recovery, faster optimisation when the estimator is used for preconditioning, and the utility of the estimated GGN for early stopping.


### [74] [Instrumental Variables in Continuous-Time Stochastic Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07674)
*Lars Pennig, Søren Wengel Mogensen, Niki Kilbertus*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Causal questions often arise in systems that evolve in continuous time. While instrumental variable (IV) methods provide a principled approach to identify causal effects in static or discrete-time settings, even in the presence of unobserved confounding, they do not yet extend to continuous-time dynamics. In this work, we develop an IV framework for stationary continuous-time stochastic processes parameterized by stochastic differential equations (SDE), where the instrument itself takes the form of a stochastic process. We encode the structural sparsity and causal effects directly at the infinitesimal level of the drift, and contrast these assumptions with the IV assumptions on the discretized version of the same process. Based on these structural assumptions, we derive moment restrictions using path-valued instruments. We show how these restrictions simplify for Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes and present an identification condition together with a corresponding consistent estimation method. Finally, we extend our estimation framework to nonlinear causal effects by expressing the path-based moment restrictions leveraging the signature kernel. Empirically, our method recovers causal effects in confounded continuous-time systems where maximum-likelihood-based estimation and discrete-time IV methods are systematically biased.


### [75] [$O(1/n)$ Convergence Rate for RVI $Q$-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07676)
*Avik Kar, Siddharth Chandak, Rahul Singh, Shalabh Bhatnagar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We establish finite-time convergence guarantee for synchronous relative value iteration (RVI) $Q$-learning for finite weakly communicating Markov decision processes (MDPs). For stochastic Krasnosel'ski\uı--Mann iterations driven by a non-expansive, linearly regular map on a finite-dimensional normed space, we establish last-iterate mean-squared residual bounds under martingale-difference noise whose conditional second moment grows at most linearly with the squared residual. Suitable polynomial stepsizes of order $n^{-a}$ yield an $O(n^{-a})$ rate for $0<a\leq1$. We verify linear regularity and the noise condition for the centered Bellman operator for finite weakly communicating MDPs. Applying the general result gives an $O(n^{-1})$ bound on the mean-squared Bellman span residual of RVI $Q$-learning iterates when a suitable stepsize sequence is used. The positive gap between optimal and suboptimal stationary deterministic policies then yields an $O(n^{-1})$ bound on the expected suboptimality of the greedy policy. Consequently, $O(\epsilon^{-1})$ synchronous iterations suffice for an $\epsilon$-optimal policy in expectation. These guarantees are instance-dependent, with constants that depend on the hitting-time parameters and the suboptimality gap of the second-best policy.


### [76] [Non-Parametric Mutual Information Estimation with Vine Copulas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07693)
*Sigurd Holmsen, Berit Omli Øksnes, Ingrid Hobæk Haff, Sylvia Richardson, Ali Ramezani-Kebrya*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce VINE: a non-parametric mutual information (MI) estimator based on simplified D-vine copulas. Approximating the joint density with a simplified D-vine allows the density ratio to simplify to a product of pair copulas, which we can estimate non-parametrically. This structural simplification yields VINE, a flexible and computationally efficient MI estimator. We compare VINE against SotA neural MI estimators on a range of datasets, both under settings where the simplified D-vine assumption holds and where it is violated, demonstrating competitive accuracy at a lower computational cost for low- to medium-dimensional datasets.


### [77] [Equivariant Deep Learning in Direct-Product Space for Electronic-Structure Hamiltonian Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07695)
*Shi Yin, Zujian Dai, Xinyang Pan, Lixin He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Most existing equivariant architectures for Hamiltonian prediction propagate features in direct-sum spaces of irreducible spherical tensors, whereas the target Hamiltonian is naturally an operator block in a direct-product representation over local orbital and spin bases. This representation mismatch fragments contiguous Hamiltonian blocks, obscures direct manipulation of orbital couplings, and organizes features into irregular angular-momentum components that require numerous transformations and are poorly aligned with efficient hardware execution and memory management. To address both the representation mismatch and computational bottleneck, we propose DP-Learner, an equivariant learning framework that performs message passing directly on Hamiltonian-like operator blocks in the direct-product space. Theoretically, we propose Lie-generator-based directional embedding and invariantly weighted left--right products and contractions that preserve equivariance to SO(3), SU(2), and spatial inversions, without explicit Clebsch--Gordan decomposition or inverse reconstruction. Methodologically, we instantiate this principle as a Transformer architecture, in which Hamiltonian-like feature matrices or tensors evolve through equivariant operators in the direct-product space, while non-linear invariant scalar routing controls structured gating over matrix units, directional propagation, attention, and environment-embedded context interactions. We further construct MP-HAM-110K, a large-scale Hamiltonian dataset comprising more than 110,000 material structures covering 68 elements with both Hamiltonian and wavefunction data. Experiments on MP-HAM-110K and Materials-HAM-SOC demonstrate that DP-Learner dramatically improves Hamiltonian prediction accuracy and inference efficiency over the state-of-the-art method.


### [78] [Evaluating Language Model Features for Zero-Shot Time-Series Anomaly Detection Under Isolation Assumption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07720)
*Romain Hermary, Nesryne Mejri, Djamila Aouada*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Time-Series Anomaly Detection (TSAD) is a fundamentally challenging task: it is unsupervised, requires high local precision capabilities, and suffers from limited labelled data. Recent work has explored adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to time-series via cross-modal transfer. However, existing approaches are poorly suited to Multivariate TSAD (MTSAD), as tokenisation often obscures fine-grained temporal and variables structures, and most methods optimise auxiliary objectives such as forecasting or reconstruction. We posit that these surrogate objectives become unreliable as model capacity increases. Highly expressive models risk reconstructing or forecasting anomalies, undermining the foundations of both strategies. Instead, we propose to use LLMs purely as feature extractors and directly analyse their induced feature spaces. In this work, investigate the ability of LLMs to provide useful representations for MTSADs in a zero-shot setting through classification. To this end, we study the separability of the extracted features, under the assumption that anomalous features should be isolated from normal ones. We analyse their temporal and contextual discriminative ability using simple step-wise tokenisation schemes, with one time-step per token, which preserve fine-grained local information. Their usability for MTSAD is assessed using an unsupervised Isolation Forest, enabling a direct evaluation of the resulting representation quality. We further investigate whether the observed performance can be attributed to the language-related pre-training of the LLMs or arises independently of it. Our results provide empirical insight into the ability of LLMs to represent multivariate time-series for anomaly detection and highlight broader applications of cross-modal foundation models.


### [79] [Reinforcing What the Student Has Learned: Teacher-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07722)
*Bozhen Fang, Linhao Zhong, Xuehao Wang, Huanyi Zheng, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model reasoning through dense teacher feedback on student-generated this http URL first observe an amplitude disparity in this feedback: on math reasoning responses, style-related positions exhibit 3.5 times the mean absolute teacher--student log-probability difference of mathematical this http URL OPD updates by these discrepancies thus directs much of the update strength toward matching the teacher's expression. We instead take the student's own pretrained base as a reference: the student--base difference records the token preferences acquired during post-training and provides supervision that does not depend on the student's distance from the teacher. Building on this reference, we propose Student-Delta On-Policy Distillation (SD-OPD), which uses centered student--base differences as update coefficients, keeps each coefficient only when its sign agrees with centered teacher--student feedback, and recomputes the student signal every training round. Across 5 teacher--student configurations with Qwen3 students of 1.7B to 8B parameters, SD-OPD improves six-task mathematics average pass@1 (avg@16) over OPD by 7.76 to 10.37 percentage points. Code is available at this https URL .


### [80] [Static Recovery Is Not Dynamic Stability: Dynamics-Aware Benchmarking of Protein Motif Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07723)
*Emile de Bruyn, Moritz Schäffler, Jannik Schneider, Sebastian R. Schmidt, Chanwoong Hwang, Aleena Siji, Utkarsh Upadhyay, Stefan Bauer, Alexander Schug, Stefan Kesselheim, Artur Yakimovich, Alina Bazarova*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Protein motif scaffolding benchmarks assess whether generative models can build scaffolds around functionally important residues. Their conclusions depend on which motifs are tested and which criteria define success. Existing benchmarks typically use small hand-curated motif sets and static refolding criteria, but protein function often depends on conformational ensembles and transitions. Here, we address both benchmark scope and success criteria. We introduce BILBY-PRO, a systematically derived benchmark of structurally conserved functional motifs from PROSITE-linked experimental structures, comprising 220 cases from 174 PROSITE motif-pattern entries. Evaluating six recent scaffold-generation models reveals distinct patterns in target coverage and unique-solution yield: RFDiffusion and Proteina solve more targets, whereas ESM3 produces more unique successful scaffolds on the targets it solves. We further introduce BILBY-MD, a reference-based all-atom molecular dynamics evaluation framework. Applied to statically solved designs from the original MotifBench benchmark, it compares motif drift, scaffold drift, motif-contact preservation, and motif-dihedral free-energy landscapes against reference simulations. Of 55 statically solved model-problem pairs, 31 satisfy our MD-supported criterion and only 8 pass all checks. Compact, self-contained motifs are often MD-supported, whereas interface-like or context-dependent motifs fail more frequently. Static refolding success therefore provides useful evidence of local motif recovery but does not establish dynamical fidelity of the full scaffold. BILBY highlights three complementary axes of motif-scaffolding evaluation: target coverage, unique-solution yield, and MD-supported dynamical fidelity.


### [81] [Comparing Classification and Feature Learning by Analyzing Their Loss Functions in Normalized Spaces with Different Scale Factors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07741)
*Qiufu Li, Huibin Xiao, Linlin Shen, Weijia Fan, Xi Jia, Yongdong Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Classification and feature learning are two closely interrelated fundamental tasks. Feature learning in supervised settings is typically achieved by training classification models, under the common assumption that higher classification accuracy implies better feature quality. In this paper, we first point out that classification accuracy reflects the sample-wise local properties of a model on a dataset, whereas feature learning aims to learn features with good dataset-level global properties, such as intra-class compactness and inter-class separability. From the first principle of feature learning, we redesign the binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, a counterpart of the cross-entropy (CE) loss used in classification. Then, in normalized feature space, we analyze the minima of the BCE and CE losses and show that both can lead to neural collapse, where sample features from different classes become maximally compact and maximally separated. More importantly, by investigating the convergence behaviors of the two losses as the scale factor $\gamma$ varies in the normalized space, we reveal how classification accuracy and feature quality change differently across spaces of different sizes. Under a fixed training strategy, a very large $\gamma$ achieves perfect classification accuracy but slows convergence toward the ideal feature geometry, whereas a very small $\gamma$ makes classification highly sensitive to small perturbations. A moderate $\gamma$ is therefore necessary for simultaneously achieving reliable classification and well-structured features. Extensive experiments validate these findings and demonstrate that BCE loss exhibits advantages in long-tail and open-set recognitions, as it better aligns with the global requirements of feature learning.


### [82] [Ordering Multivariate Time Series by Volatility Memory: A Temporal Convolutional Approach to Volatility Heredity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07746)
*Kaoutar El Azaar, Mohamed Hamlich, Moncef Garouani, Franck Ravat, Asmae Chakir, Mohamed Tabaa*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Volatility dynamics can carry information about temporal relationships that is not apparent in the conditional mean. Extracting this information is difficult because the persistence of each variable can obscure its dependence on other variables. This paper presents QS-TCN, a method for ordering multivariate time series that combines statistical estimates of volatility memory with temporal convolutional learning. Persistence estimates obtained by quasi-maximum likelihood and temporal modes extracted by the Matrix Pencil method define structured filters within the network. Together with learned filters, they represent the history of the target and support comparisons of directional evidence between variables. These comparisons are combined into a global order of precedence, which does not identify a causal graph. The method is evaluated on synthetic systems with linear and nonlinear volatility interactions and on the Finance and fMRI benchmarks, whose reference relations are known. Across the four synthetic volatility regimes studied, QS-TCN reaches a higher mean ordering quality than an external lead--lag ordering and its squared-input adaptation, although the gain over the adaptation is uncertain in two regimes. Applications to currency, gold and cryptocurrency markets are reported descriptively.


### [83] [ConvPE: Convolutional Position Embedding Improves Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07749)
*Zehao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: ConvPE (Convolutional Position Embedding) improves both the performance and efficiency of Transformer models in language modeling, long-context extension, and vision. It injects a windowed, content-dependent bias into attention scores through a lightweight causal convolution over a low-dimensional projection of the hidden states. As a lightweight plug-in, ConvPE is compatible with RoPE, attention masks, and KV concatenation. It also supports high-dimensional position embeddings via high-dimensional convolutions. In a reproduction, ConvPE outperforms five 1D baselines and eight 2D baselines. The code is publicly available at this https URL .


### [84] [Foresee to Trade: Forecast-Augmented Policy Optimization for Portfolio Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07750)
*Zeyu Liu, Xuanzhi Feng, Haoran Xu, Lai Sing Kwong, Yang Li, Hualei Zhang, Hao Chen, Jie Zhang, Xiaoyi Pang, Song Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Online investment services must translate asset opportunities into investment outcomes for users' accounts, yet the same market opportunity can warrant different account actions. We study how supervision from future asset opportunities can strengthen account-level trading decisions and propose Forecast-Augmented Relative Policy Optimization (FRPO). FRPO separately supervises asset opportunities and account decisions through a jointly learned representation: a Financial State Head (FSH) learns future relative-return targets to provide asset-level opportunity supervision, while Same-Path Group-Relative Optimization compares trading trajectories under the same market path and initial account to provide account-level relative-outcome supervision. An Adaptive Gate coordinates auxiliary supervision using predictive variance and gradient alignment. FSH is used only during training; inference uses the encoder and policy for trading. Across DJIA30, TOPIX Core30, and three investment-style portfolios, ten-seed evaluations show that FRPO achieves the highest mean cumulative and annualized returns among the compared methods in all five settings. These results support strengthening investment agents' trading decisions through joint supervision from asset opportunities and trading outcomes.


### [85] [Neural Collapse on Imbalanced Datasets and Open-Set Scenario](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07753)
*Qiufu Li, Huibing Xiao, Linlin Shen, Yongdong Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural collapse (NC) describes the elegant geometric structure that sample features and classifier vectors exhibit when a classification model's loss reaches its minimum. Class imbalance, however, generally distorts standard NC. In this paper, we theoretically reformulate NC on imbalanced datasets and in open-set settings. Our analysis is based on an optimization problem over class-wise averaged losses, rather than the global averaged losses used in prior NC studies. We prove that when class-wise averaged losses attain their minima, standard NC is achieved on balanced or imbalanced datasets, for both cross-entropy (CE) and binary cross-entropy (BCE) losses. We further extend the analysis to an open-set-inspired scenario, where some classes contain no training samples yet are still assigned classifier vectors in the losses. We find that in this scenario, when class-wise averaged losses reach their minima, standard NC still emerges on the known classes with training samples, while the classifier vectors of unknown classes without training samples overlap with each other and become orthogonal to those of the known classes. Finally, we analyze Softmax- and Sigmoid-based contrastive losses re-balanced by inverse sample frequency, proving that at their minima on balanced or imbalanced datasets, sample features of different classes likewise form standard NC.


### [86] [Scaling Laws for SAE Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07756)
*Nikita Koriagin, Nikita Balagansky, Daniil Gavrilov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Activation storage is often a bottleneck in sparse autoencoder (SAE) training. We study how many unique activations an SAE needs when its total training budget is fixed. Quality improves rapidly as the buffer grows, then reaches a plateau where replaying the buffer is nearly as effective as using fresh activations throughout training. Beyond this point, additional unique activations bring little benefit to reconstruction or interpretability, so the buffer need not grow with the training budget. We describe this tradeoff between diversity and repetition with a scaling law for limited data. We validate it across dictionary sizes and token budgets, early, middle, and late layers of Llama-3.1, Qwen3 model sizes, and BatchTopK, TopK, and JumpReLU SAEs. Sweeps using mathematical text and Python code test the effect of changing the activation source. Evaluation covers explained variance (EV), reconstruction MSE, feature absorption, automated interpretability, and sparse probing. These results give a practical training rule: choose the smallest buffer that reaches the quality plateau and replay it for the remaining budget. In our experiments, this reduces activation storage by $8\text{--}64\times$ with negligible quality loss.


### [87] [Optimal Learning in Games with Arbitrary Delays: Best-of-Both-Worlds for Self-Play and Adversarial Opponents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07773)
*Emmanuel Esposito, Andrew Jacobsen, Hao Qiu, Alexander Ryabchenko, Mengxiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study learning in zero-sum games with delayed feedback. We introduce Multi-Anchor Optimistic Online Mirror Descent (MAO-OMD), a single algorithm which obtains optimal fast rates in self-play on smooth games, while simultaneously maintaining minimax optimal guarantees against arbitrary opponents. In particular, in smooth zero-sum games with arbitrary and unknown feedback delays, MAO-OMD obtains $O(\sigma_{\max}+1)$ social and individual regrets when used by both players, where $\sigma_{\max}$ denotes the maximum number of outstanding gradients, while also guaranteeing minimax optimal $O(\sqrt{T+d_{\mathrm{tot}}})$ individual regret against arbitrary opponents, where $d_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is the total delay. Moreover, we show that these optimal rates cannot be obtained simultaneously using the standard black-box reduction to the undelayed setting. We also extend our results to multi-player games, and to games with player-dependent delay sequences.


### [88] [Adapters as Data: The Latent Space of LoRAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07775)
*Moritz Grävinghoff, Konstantin Wehmeyer, Aron Asefaw, Boris Knyazev, Damian Borth*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: LoRA adapters are usually treated as finished artifacts rather than as a data modality. Prior work has shown that LoRA adapters populate a low-dimensional manifold that a linear PCA basis already organizes well enough to support different operations such as sampling, editing, and inversion. We ask what a learned, nonlinear latent adds beyond this. We train a transformer autoencoder on the same population of LoRA identity adapters, mapping each into a latent space and compare every operation against the identical operation applied directly to the raw adapter weights. The latent preserves the capabilities already available in weight space, sampling diverse identities and editing individual attributes. Beyond preservation, we show that the latent is far more robust to displacement than weight space, degrading into distorted but still valid faces rather than texture. Fixed-dimensional weight-space representations cannot natively host adapters of more than one rank, so we extend our sequential autoencoder to hold mixed-rank adapters within one shared latent. This gives a path toward treating adapter weights of varying architecture as a single, general modality rather than one tied to a fixed rank, and points to a learned, nonlinear latent as the mechanism that could carry this generalization further, toward heterogeneous populations of adapters.


### [89] [PBPO: Prefix-Block Policy Optimization for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07783)
*Katherine Tieu, Dongqi Fu, Si Zhang, Hanqing Zeng, Tianxin Wei, Chenglin Wei, Yinglong Xia, Hong Li, Jingrui He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) commonly decode responses by proceeding autoregressively across blocks while iteratively denoising multiple tokens within each block. This structure makes block-level policy optimization a natural adaptation of autoregressive policy optimization. However, the exact likelihood of a completed block is intractable because it marginalizes over the intermediate states and reveal orders of the denoising process. To realize the block-level formulation, we construct a prefix-block state and use a single model evaluation to define a factorized one-step surrogate for the block likelihood. The resulting block surrogate ratio is a product of its token surrogate ratios, allowing variation at individual positions to propagate across the entire block before clipping. We therefore introduce Prefix-Block Policy Optimization (PBPO), which preserves block-level conditioning but applies surrogate importance weighting and clipping separately to each token. PBPO further diversifies repeated updates by resampling corrupted views of the prompt and revealed prefix, a design motivated by masked-dLLM pre-training. Under our analysis assumptions, block-product weighting amplifies the variance of gradient contributions relative to token-local weighting. Across two LLaDA-family initializations, six benchmarks, and three generation lengths, PBPO achieves competitive performance against existing dLLM RL methods.


### [90] [EnergyBench: a live, gate-aligned benchmark of day-ahead forecasts for European power markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07784)
*Evgenii Genov, Ander Martinez Alonso, Christoph Bergmeir*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Energy forecasting models are usually compared on backtests and fixed public datasets. Backtests can use data that did not yet exist when the forecast would have been made, and a fixed test set, reused across studies and possibly contained in the pretraining data of foundation models, invites overfitting to the benchmark. EnergyBench evaluates every entry live instead. Each day at 11:30 CET/CEST, thirty minutes before the day-ahead (SDAC) gate closes, every model forecasts the next day's price, wind and solar generation and load on the 15-minute grid for six bidding zones (BE, NL, FR, DE-LU, DK1, DK2). Unlike existing live benchmarks, EnergyBench runs the models itself, on one information set that it freezes at the cut-off and publishes, including three numerical weather prediction models, so that foundation models can be compared with and without the same weather covariates under the actual market deadline. Errors are pooled per series against a seasonal-naive baseline, and models are ranked by a Bradley-Terry rating on the Elo scale with a day-bootstrap interval. The field spans baselines, linear and gradient-boosted models on the frozen weather, eighteen time-series foundation models from eleven families, ensembles, and the grid operators' own forecasts. Over the first thirty-two live days, the covariates reduced the error of each of the ten foundation models run with and without them by 37-51% on matched races (64-73% on wind, 48-61% on solar). Used zero-shot, these models match or outperform a LightGBM model trained on the same covariates. Model size, from 3M to 331M parameters, has a much smaller effect, and median ensembles of the covariate models rank highest. The grid operators' pre-gate forecasts remain ahead on wind and solar, where the best single foundation models have 16-22% more error; on load the gap is 4-6%, within the intervals.


### [91] [Computation, not weight access: large language models as auditors of clinical prediction networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07847)
*Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Frontier large language models (LLMs) have been reported to read, edit and write the weights of small neural networks, raising the prospect of auditing clinical prediction models from their files alone. Public demonstrations, however, mixed reading raw weights with running code. We separated these conditions in internally pre-specified simulation experiments. Tanh networks of 105 to 38,401 weights were trained on simulated cohorts of patients with subarachnoid haemorrhage, each with a planted relationship or none, and one network was trained on labels that under-recorded deaths in a subgroup. GPT-6 Astra and four other GPT models answered single prompts under a default or an explicit system instruction, either with the weights in the prompt and no tools, or with a calculation-only tool that exposed the weights (white box) or only the network's predictions (black box). Without tools, Astra recovered planted relationships in 353-weight networks but became unreliable at 3,457 weights, and under the default instruction it declined 11 of 12 audit and repair tasks as unidentifiable; the explicit instruction removed these refusals. With the tool, it recovered the planted relationship in all 29 runs on networks of up to 38,401 weights, raised no false alarm in 15 runs on null networks, and found the biased subgroup in 6 of 6 runs. Black-box access performed as well as white-box access, and other GPT-6 models read as accurately. A well-designed script matched the comparator models on the audit and the best LLM repair. For auditing these networks, what mattered was computation and elicitation, not access to the weights.


### [92] [When, Where, How: Active In-Context Learning for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07886)
*Christian M. M. Frey*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) adapt to a new task only through the labeled rows in their context. However, labels are expensive to acquire in real-world scenarios due to the need for highly specific domain knowledge to ensure high-quality data. Active in-context learning transfers classical acquisition rules to this setting, in which the model that guides acquisition changes with every label added. We ask how the value of diversity, predictive uncertainty and target-aware information gain changes as a frozen TFM acquires more context. For that, we study Target-Aware Nearest Neighbor (TANN) acquisition and the Expected Predictive Information Gain in Context (EPIC) and evaluate them against classical rules. Our study across twenty benchmarks with two well-established TFMs and classical GBDTs highlights three findings: (i) When: diversity-based acquisition is best during the first acquisition iterations; (ii) Where: labeling pool rows that serve the target inputs via a simple target-aware nearest-neighbor rule matters substantially; (iii) How: EPIC yields the best models at the label budget, retains on average 80--87% of the chance-corrected full-data performance with only 5-10% of the pool labeled on the eight benchmarks with the largest pools, and selects labels that also improve the predictive performance of gradient-boosted decision trees.


### [93] [DISCERN: Distributional Synthetic Replay for Retain-Free Incremental Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07892)
*Nicolás Segura-Kunsagi, Juan Luis Suárez-Díaz, Iván Sevillano-García, Francisco Herrera*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of specific training data from a trained model without retraining from scratch. In real deployments, however, deletion requests arrive over time, calling for incremental class unlearning (ICU): classes are removed sequentially, previously forgotten knowledge must remain forgotten, and the remaining classes must be preserved. We present DIstributional SynthetiC rEplay for Retain-free iNcremental unlearning (DISCERN), an intervention-based class unlearning method that replaces access to the retain set with synthetic embeddings sampled from per-class parametric distributions. Unlike prototype-based replay, which assumes an isotropic Gaussian around each class centroid, DISCERN fits Euclidean (Gaussian, Laplace, Student's $t$) and directional (von Mises--Fisher, Fisher--Bingham) families to each class and selects among them through statistical tests and, when none of them accepts, cross-validated log-likelihood. This design is motivated by the architecture-dependent latent geometry: transformer embeddings lie approximately on a hypersphere and concentrate around class directions, unlike those of convolutional networks. We evaluate DISCERN against seven retain-free baselines on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 with two convolutional and two transformer backbones, under single-class, multi-class, and incremental unlearning. DISCERN is competitive when classes are removed at once and is the most stable method in ICU on transformers: on ViT-B/16, after 20 sequential deletions it retains a harmonic mean of $0.797$, whereas the strongest method in the simultaneous setting drops from $0.918$ to $0.048$. A paired ablation shows that per-class distribution selection substantially reduces the resurfacing of previously forgotten classes, acting as a stabilizer of accumulated forgetting rather than a uniform improvement.


### [94] [Same Answer, Different Future: Matched Predictions Do Not Imply Matched Belief Updating in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07913)
*Weihan Li, Junhao Wu, Xinlei Chen, Tianshi Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Language models can produce similar probabilistic predictions after observing outcomes, reading summaries, receiving instructions, or undergoing activation interventions. Whether these routes induce equivalent belief states remains unclear: agreement in current predictions does not establish how a model will respond to subsequent evidence. We investigate this distinction in a Bernoulli inference task with an analytic Bayesian reference. Across three open-weight language models, we prepare states through four textual routes and two activation-level interventions, measure both next-outcome continuations and explicit probabilistic estimates, and append identical new evidence from each prepared state. We quantify the persistence of the initial belief through its effective evidence weight, the number of new observations it is worth. We find that the format of evidence decides which readouts express its content: outcome sequences primarily affect continuations, whereas summaries and stated rates primarily affect explicit estimates. Moreover, effective weights differ substantially across routes, with summaries receiving weights that scale weakly with their stated observation counts and elicitation questions changing inferred weights by orders of magnitude. In a one-shot steering setting, an intervention that reproduces a natural record's immediate prediction loses most of its effect under subsequent evidence. These results show that agreement in current readouts is insufficient to establish equivalence in belief updating: how a belief is acquired and how it is read jointly determine its subsequent dynamics.


### [95] [Flux as a Codec: Zero-Shot Lossy Compression with Flux](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07929)
*Fuma Kimishima, Jinjia Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We propose FluxC, a zero-shot codec that uses Flux as a compression prior. Although DiffC has shown that pretrained diffusion models can serve as zero-shot codecs, we ask how the choice of coding path and reconstruction method affects rate--distortion--perception behavior. We analyze FluxC along three axes: coding cost and bit allocation, reconstruction method, and low rate distortion. At matched signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), we show that the path choice does not affect the ideal information rate, but changes how bits are allocated across time steps.
